 Unrevealed Whispers
 Review: Blazej Malinowski steps away from the dancefloor on the second release in Inner Tension Extra's series. Nocturnal Shadows explores a darker, more introspective side of his sound. Across this collection of ambient and experimental works, atmosphere leads over rhythm, with shifting textures and silence shaping an uneasy and dreamlike world. The music feels suspended between wakefulness and sleep - the familiar place where thoughts spiral and something unsettling lurks beneath. It's a mad departure from the club that requires you to live comfortably with uncertainty rather than seeking a resolution. Great for the hours when sleep evades you. … Read more

 Amen II (22:03)
 Review: MYS and London-based producer Big Hands come together on 'Amen' to explore the religiously loaded word's passage between the sacred and the everyday and use it to open "a gateway into a timeless, transcultural dimension. The music was recorded during a three-hour improvised session at Frequenze Studio in Monza and built around a continuous, immersive flow over conventional song structure. 'Amen I' is a meditation on sombre chords and the big impact leaving plenty of open space can have.  Ringar Sa Som Pa Vatten (11:44)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1180582-01-02-02.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1180582-01-02-02.mp3] 
 Tystnaden Pa Fabriksg (7:14)
 Review: Sweden is one of Europe's foremost countries for musical innovation and the formation of new ideas and sounds. Anders Olofsson is just one case in point. Developed while based inside one of several condemned buildings on Fabriksgatan, Gothenburg, like so many good things, here the concept is rooted in necessity - specifically, the need to drown out the traffic below. A work of real ambient thought, the tones, sonic shapes and keys that make up Stiltje have their foundations in the found noises outside those windows, repurposed, re-realised and reprocessed into things that would soften the street level busyness, render it almost silent, and certainly calmer. Hearing it as released, the record is so delicate and quietly immersive you can hardly believe the origin story.

 Pomegranate (2:34)
 Review: Bibio and Dorian Concept's decade-long friendship gives Answers a sense of ease that a first-time pairing rarely achieves. Built entirely remotely between Wales and Austria, the record reflects both artists' preference for a patient and solitary process. Bibio's tactile, tape-warmed guitar work meets Dorian Concept's fluid, improvisational synth playing in a way that feels like a natural exchange between mates, conversational rather than assembled from separate parts. Their previous collaborations, including Bibio's 'Hide' rework, laid real groundwork for this partnership. It's a rich, texture-forward instrumental record that plays less like a debut and more like two old friends in full flow. … Read more

 Erik Satie, Priere C1895 (1:43)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1185005-01-02-07.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1185005-01-02-07.mp3] 
 Alma Mahler, 5 Lieder, No. 1, 'die Stille Stadt' 1910 (4:31)
 Review: Double bass, electric guitar, flute, melodica. Some vocals. And subtle analogue synthesisers. A lot of kit that's just about still going, chosen as much for the inimitable timbre as usability. Cop Tears is about as human a record as you're likely to stumble upon, recorded at home in New York City in what we can only imagine was a slow, patient series of sessions. Classical, medieval at times, but strangely very clearly 21st Century in finish, the tracks here span centuries, from the 15th to a decade ago, the album acting as an aural highway through the time-space continuum that shows just how connected different eras and ages are. Providing you're listening to the right parts, of course. Unique would be one word to put it.

 Bloom (instrumental) (7:07)
 Review: By 2001, Suffolk-born Brian Eno had the odd problem of sounding like a reference point to his own imitators. Drawn From Life, made with German composer J Peter Schwalm, mostly dodges that trap through its string writing. 'Persis' circles a dark ostinato that keeps needling at the electronics, then Laurie Anderson's vocodered intonations make 'Like Pictures Part #2' feel slightly unreal. The beats can be almost comically polite but 'Bloom' gets the balance right. Elegant stuff, with just enough strangeness left in the seams. … Read more

 James Asher - "Micro Life" (2:04)
 Review: Made purely as functional music library material for nature documentaries, Nature's World transcends that utilitarian origin surprisingly well. James Harpham and James Asher's synth-driven cues are nice and strange within their tight, bite-sized runtimes. 'Aquarium' drifts into real underwater haze, while 'Catacombs' descends somewhere considerably darker than most library work of the era dared to go. 'Flower Garden' remains the obvious standout, its synth-funk groove infectious enough that Knxwledge later sampled it outright. There's real playfulness too with 'Jumbo Jive' scoring what's essentially a comical mating dance. … Read more

 Review: Bristol's James McKeown returns to Library of the Occult with the sonic equivalent of finding a mossy door where there definitely wasn't one before. 'Ockham' lets in a little daylight, though even that feels provisional. Much of it was made on a Teenage Engineering EP-1320, which feels wonderfully apt: expensive little gadget, medieval seance. … Read more

 Orphic Hymn (3:35)
 Review: Late Icelandic composer Johann Johannsson's Orphee returns for its tenth anniversary. Inspired by the myth of Orpheus, it traces a path through death, rebirth and the slippery nature of memory by weaving sparse strings, organ, electronics and ghostly vocal passages into something spellbinding. There's an intimacy to it but also a sense of vast, unknowable space that spreads out all around you. Fragments of sound appear and disappear like half-remembered dreams and a decade on, it is still contemplative and beautiful. … Read more

 Review: Short Span's eleventh and weightiest release so far, and their first double LP: the debut album from K Wata, a New York producer who's been placing carefully on Anno and City-2 St Giga, and noted for his production contrubtions to Yaeji's With a Hammer and Daytimers collaborative moves.  Wimborn (1994 unreleased remix - 10") (12:09)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1161192-01-06-01.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1161192-01-06-01.mp3] 
 Wimborn (1994 unreleased Ambient remix) (7:17)
 Review: Originally released in 1994 and now returning in an expanded edition on Forever, Io is one of the more distinctive ambient works to emerge from Britain's experimental electronic underground of the early nineties. Created by Jon Tye - later to become boss of Lo Recordings - and Pete Smith under the MLO name, the album is a fascinating listen that finds itself between modern composition, environmental sound design and exploratory electronics. A very relaxing album also has has romantic nostalgic moments of yesterday's chill out scene as well. Across its eight original pieces, Io unfolds with patience and purpose. The music develops through slowly shifting harmonies, delicate tonal changes and carefully layered textures, with echoes of Erik Satie and Claude Debussy audible in the spacious arrangements, while the influence of John Cage appears in the use of silence, unpredictability and subtle sonic detail. MLO create an beautiful atmosphere across these eight pieces, as tracks such as 'Moon Over Lagos' and 'Ghost' have warmth yet mystery, while the two-part 'In Search Of Lost Time' demonstrates the duo's skill for gradual development. This reissue presents the original album across a beautifully packaged double LP and adds a limited 10" featuring two previously unreleased versions of 'Wimborne'. It remains a rewarding and remarkably timeless listening experience over 30 years later. … Read more

 Review: As Museum Of No Art, artist and musician Mona Steinwidder turns two years of exploring clarinet and woodwind into A Serpent Called Emit, an unpredictable set that finds beauty in the space between simplicity and complexity.  Rame Head (4:03)
 Review: Jon Tye has spent three decades making gentle, optimistic electronics in various projects from his 1990s group MLO to his Ocean Moon solo alias and his work running Lo Recordings. Silver Ley, formerly Bethany Ley, is a Bristol harpist and vocalist who has worked with Ishmael Ensemble, Batu and Alabaster De Plume, and Prismatic Fields is their first record together. Glossy harp glissandos, synth waves and enchanted vocals all drift through a fantasy-leaning dreamscape here that will bring to mind genre greats like Boards of Canada. It's functional music for calming minds and feels formless, as if it's always playing somewhere rather than having been created for this album. … Read more

 A Lonesome Sentiment (6:19)
 Review: Almost 20 years on, Washington-based Drew Sullivan's 2007 debut as Slow Dancing Society hasn't dated the way you'd expect. The Sound Of Lights When Dim is a reserved work with reverberating guitar drifting through broad, unhurried tones. On opener 'Be There' reversed guitar and blurred vocal traces dissolve before they resolve on 'The Delicate Sound Of Silence.' It's deeply emotional ambient with endless oceanic scale and plenty of encouragement to reflect on your own. The glowing synths, patient guitar work and preoccupation with memory that would define Sullivan's next two decades are all present from the start and still have the same lasting impact. … Read more

 The Hum III (reprise)
 Review: Sam Beste's CV is pretty long for someone so young. As The Vernon Spring he makes restrained, muted piano music, and A Plane Over Woods adds voices, samples and looped vinyl crackle to that base style. Beste is best known as part of the alt-soul trio Hejira and has worked with Amy Winehouse, Kano and Blood Orange, produced the string duo Fran & Flora, and played hang drum for Portico Quartet. This record was his first long-form solo statement and five years on it warrants a reissue as the blend of intimacy and texture still sounds fresh. Though quiet, it is far from timid. … Read more

 Effacing The Ephemeral (10:05)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1183288-01-02-01.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1183288-01-02-01.mp3] 
 Eclipsing Your Earth (5:18)
 [link: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1183288-01-02-02.mp3] [audio: https://www.juno.co.uk/MP3/SF1183288-01-02-02.mp3] 
 Erasing Empires (14:06)
 Review: zake and bvdub are towering figures in the world of ambient - and prolific ones at that. The pair now return to zake's hugely influential Past Inside the Present label with the four-track long player Erasure. Each piece goes typically long and builds slowly but surely into an all-consuming soundscape despite the fact each one is crafted simply from textured synth drift. There's distortion brewing under 'Effacing The Ephemeral' that nods to the likes of Stephen O'Malley while 'Eclipsing Your Earth' is a much more luminous and celestial mood that empties your mind of everything. 'Erasing Empires' then builds to an intense crescendo before falling away to sultry strings. A hugely dramatic work.
 … Read more

 These cookies are required to deliver the service or functionality you request. They enable core features such as page navigation, secure access, authentication, and load balancing. Without them, the website cannot function properly. Consent is not required for these cookies.
